- madam president, i would like to draw your attention to the fact that the council is violating the rules of procedure - and not for the first time. we as members of parliament have the opportunity once a month to put a written question with priority, which, according to the rules of procedure, must be answered within three weeks. the council is now increasingly failing to meet this deadline. the most recent example is this: in october i submitted a question which has not been answered to date, namely, when does the council intend to finally implement the promised increase in europol staff salaries! this is not an isolated incident, but one that is becoming more frequent. i urge you to urge the council to respect the rules of procedure. (applause from the centre)
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. we all agree that the directive on the restructuring of credit institutions is long overdue. that is why we welcome them. finally, a uniform procedure is established and coherence is established between the reorganisation procedure and the insolvency regulation. it should be stressed that the provisions on the protection of creditors' rights are particularly important here, since they lay down the information to be given to creditors and the conditions for their payment. this ensures equal treatment of all creditors in liquidation proceedings, including those in countries other than the home member state. the text is quite good in its present form, but there were some ambiguities. the commission has also adopted a number of amendments to the committee's rules, including a number of amendments to the rules of procedure, which strengthen the overriding principle of universality and remove certain incompatibilities with the regulation on financial conglomerates that could lead to legal uncertainty. you have already noticed in the debate that we have different views on one point, namely, in particular, on amendments nos 3 and 5. the close parallelism between the restructuring and liquidation of credit institutions and insurance companies is not desirable in all aspects of this procedure. there are different views on this because many people think that these are different products, sometimes different markets, and that different products and different markets should be treated differently and not automatically equally. the common position is, in my view, balanced and has been improved in part by the amendments of the committee and the rapporteur, whom i thank, and the text should therefore be adopted as soon as possible and implemented by the member states. i regret that the dispute in the council has led to this delay of more than a decade. i would point out that the commission's first proposal was published in 1985 and the first reading of this house was held in 1987. i stress this also because in our debates - especially in the wake of the nice european council - we are constantly discussing the balance between the european institutions and in many cases we are discussing the need to speed up procedures. this is not just the case for parliament, as is often portrayed in public, although of course we can also go into more detail and speed up certain things. but this isn't about blame. the most important potential for speeding up procedures between the european institutions is undoubtedly the opinion-forming and decision-making process in the european council. this was again very clear. the commission proposal aims to ensure that a credit institution and its subsidiary in another member state can be wound up and liquidated in accordance with the principle of unity and universality. this is what this proposal for a directive provides.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. i said in the debate on 5 july on 'evaluating the quality of school education' that it is in the classrooms of today, and not just in the classrooms, that the foundations of the future of young people are being laid. education, i.e. education, training and further education, must clearly be at the centre of all policy areas. education is not an end in itself, but a prerequisite for our economic and personal survival. the transition to the knowledge society, the globalisation of the economy, social, democratic and environmental change, and the profound changes in the world of work, not least because of the new technologies, are the major challenges of the third millennium, which also require new efforts in the field of education. i therefore welcome this report again in the european parliament's plenary, because the aim of this report is to improve school education and the evaluation of its quality. quality comparisons - whether internal or external - create competition, and if this raises the quality of education in european schools and also addresses the triple challenge of the information society, globalisation and rapid scientific and technological change, then this report has achieved its objective of giving a new impetus to educational efforts and a significant step has been taken at european level. let us therefore help make the implementation report which the commission is to present to the european parliament in three years' time a success. but let me end by saying something fundamental about the future of education, because i just came from an education symposium/federal congress of the austrian people's party. education is more than employability. it must aim at developing the whole personality of the young person. this comprehensive personality development includes basic knowledge, skills and competences, the transmission of values, musical and creative education, and health and sports education. second, a good knowledge base, which teaches what is important and leaves out what is not important, is still the basis for education in the 21st century. it is therefore an understatement to say that knowledge and information should only be obtained from the internet. if you don't have basic knowledge, you don't know where to get it. this knowledge base includes cultural techniques, reading, arithmetic and writing. the new technologies offer new opportunities for acquiring knowledge and applying cultural techniques. only those who have basic knowledge can properly handle, distinguish and differentiate knowledge in this age of the knowledge explosion. this report is therefore an essential contribution to making knowledge the centre of our policies.
- mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. the world heritage, we have just heard the figures, is relatively unequally distributed. the majority of them are here in decision-making or selection process any easier. there are more refusals than commitments, and i think it is necessary to redefine the criteria as well. the diversity of cultural heritage underlines the identity of the regions themselves, and therefore it is primarily their responsibility to take it on and to carry it out, although it must be supported from outside, by the european union and by many other partners. these regional responsibilities include, among others, home and custom care, development of one's own regions and the like. i believe that these are criteria which must be met primarily in the regions. world cultural sites go beyond regional development. they are cultural treasures, witnesses to epochal changes and the uniqueness of nature. they have an extraordinary universal value. the architectural heritage, the cultural landscape and the urban heritage are interrelated, but they are incomplete as a whole unless they also include monuments of contemporary history, for example from the world of work, such as the development of working life, the design of factories, machinery and so on. they show the development of the work process of our document and in the world. the plundering of architectural treasures must therefore be combated as well as the thoughtless destruction of traces of social institutions. the rapid technological development and environmental change make it even more clear how important it is to be mindful of our natural heritage and to take responsibility for it, i.e. to protect it. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. the selection of world heritage sites should therefore be based on a balance between historical sites, religious monuments, the different religions of prehistory, contemporary history and natural heritage, and the criteria for evaluation should be modified in this direction. the preservation of cultural heritage is not only a matter of great responsibility, but also of great cost. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

hatzidakis, who has also supported us with his advice and action and his confidence in the rapporteurs. i also mention that it was a difficult birth because it took a long time, longer than it should have. but since there are constant rumors that parliament is so slow and hesitant and that we need fast track procedures and other procedures, i would like to point out that we had the first reading on 10 march 1999 but did not receive the common position until 13 april, that is more than a year later, despite the fact that it was tabled in the committees by mr jarzembowski and myself. we then also worked quickly on the common position on 5 july and decided to amend it on 5 july. the council has again taken a relatively long time to reach its opinion. i want to make a point . parliament works quickly when necessary, thanks in particular to the good cooperation i have had with mr jarzembowski. it is the council which always has great difficulty in reaching a common position. what does this railway package include? i believe that these are very important steps, namely the steps themselves, which are fixed, but also the look into the future which goes with them. first, something i would call tailor-made liberalisation, i.e. liberalisation which does not simply go across the board, which does not go beyond what reasonable, discerning experts think, but which is targeted. i would also like to thank mr jarzembowski and a few others for their efforts. you could have been braver. but whatever the case, the first steps have been taken and they should be taken, because i too believe that what is in the text can be implemented even earlier than what we have agreed, which is the minimum for me. secondly, the cost structure. it is true that we have said on the one hand that in the short term - we have followed the example of the commission - only the marginal costs, i.e. the cost of an additional train on a given route, can be charged. but in the long run, the goal must be to achieve full cost recovery here. however, this means that this cost recovery can be achieved not only by rail but also by road. the environmental costs must therefore also be taken into account, which - although mr jarzembowski still does not quite believe this, but he is half convinced - are certainly clearly higher on the road than on the railway. thirdly, we have not abolished the exception for individual member states. it hurts, but it's a pain we can take. i think the train of time will pass over it, too. i have succeeded in introducing or reintroducing a certain principle, and i thank you for your support, namely that public services may be given priority to a limited extent and for good reason, without, of course, hindering the most important part of transport, namely economic transport, goods transport, which is the most important part, because we want to move it from the road to the railway. we have also achieved that the duration of framework contracts that railways receive for the use of infrastructure is designed in such a way that the investments made by railway companies are also really paid off and implemented. i think that this is a comprehensive package with good provisions. we can say that we will be happy to go home tomorrow after the votes. each one did his part. but it certainly is not. two things are very important: firstly, the commissioner has also helped us by making it quite clear that further steps will be proposed by the commission. as i said, i hope they're tailored and targeted, but that's important. the second point, which is also very important, is that, as i have already said, we must gradually move towards a system of internalising external costs, i.e. they must be taken into account in parallel for road and rail. then, too, part of the distortion that still exists, namely the unfair treatment of rail versus road, will be eliminated. there will be no major changes overnight. so the freighters have nothing to fear. but the railways will and must also experience justice step by step and thus indirectly a promotion which is really only the creation of a just relationship. the question of labour law and working hours is also important. here too, the treatment is unfair. here too, the commissioner has promised, and the council has already decided in principle, to help us to put an end to the unfair pricing of fares and rates in the member states of the european union - this is particularly frequently practised by the willi betz company - so that we can have a fairer system here, also in terms of labour law and working time, because here too the railways are at a disadvantage compared with the legal, but above all also the factual conditions on the road. of course there must be investment in the railways. it's not enough to say, okay, we have a great system now, but nobody's investing. these investments are necessary. i say this particularly because we are about to enlarge. if the enlargement is linked to the fact that we will have additional massive truck traffic on our roads, then there will be resistance to it. if enlargement is linked to a fairer and therefore more environmentally friendly transport system, then it will be more widely accepted. that is how i understand our railway package that we are proposing today - that we have a bit more environmentally friendly transport and therefore also a bit more justice in the transport system!

the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we are now faced with the single-line bus directive, which we have already dealt with in the first reading. the rapporteur was mr murphy murphy's law is, i think, well known, but mr murphy has behaved in an exemplary manner because he too has recognised that we want to enforce important political principles with this directive, namely access for the elderly, children and disabled people. we are in complete agreement on this. we must do something for these groups: buses and public transport must be accessible to them, and we want to work together with all our strength to achieve this. why do we reject this directive? because the commission had now put forward a proposal at first reading of some 100 pages containing detailed rules, and all the groups agreed during the first reading debate to reject this directive on grounds of principle. now the commission and the council are coming back with a common position, not 100 pages, but 150! we said in the first reading that we could imagine three pages at most with the main political principles; now, there are some real goodies in this directive that you rarely find. for example, on page 17 we have figure 17: it concerns the permissible narrowing of the rear corner seats, the visibility of the prescribed area of the seat both rear corner seats, see annex i, section 7.7.8.6.3.4, and it prescribes a radius of not more than 150 millimetres in the vehicle structure. i know that in the european union we have already set the radius of curvature for bananas and cucumbers and what else, but that we are now being asked to set a radius of curvature for the single european bus is beyond my imagination! the directive also contains a formula which even engineers may find difficult to understand. i must say that this paper is not suitable for a political decision! the fact is that the designers, the bus buyers, the consumer protection organisations and also the disabled people's associations are all very unhappy with this directive, although they agree with it. everyone says that we want to achieve the goal of disabled-accessible buses in europe. we in the epp want a new approach in this area, which simply means limiting ourselves to the main political content. first, there are standards for access for the elderly, children and the disabled. this is very important and must be ensured. secondly, we want standards for the safety of the driver and his passengers. thirdly, we want the principle of mutual recognition. the principle of mutual recognition is so important because, of course, a bus in a poor little village in sicily, where it's very hot, has to look different from a bus in rush hour in london, where there are millions of passengers and the demands and dynamics of the bus are very different, or a bus in northern finland, which is used in extreme cold and for long distances and looks very different. the epp therefore will ask for the common position to be rejected and for a vote to be taken. we believe it is important that we do not want to make europe a level playing field. the european citizen is also opposed to this equalization. we want mutual recognition. i have confidence in british, french and german buses. and so it shall be in the future!

in writing. - (de) i support the telecommunications package because it has many strengths. the growing demand for frequencies, whether from telecommunications, radio, traffic, the police, the armed forces or the scientific world, simply makes a coordinated approach in europe essential in the context of international competition. in general, harmonisation at european level contributes to the creation of a continental market which can be measured to a large extent against that of american competitors. i would also like to stress the increase in administrative simplification - 8 legal instruments are being created from 20 existing legal instruments - which is a very positive development! competition rules will be the most important instrument for regulating the electronic communications market. as long as there can be no genuine competition on the market, there is a need for clear criteria for legal intervention, as well as for precise criteria and conditions for defining the obligations for guaranteeing access to and interconnection of electronic communications networks. i therefore particularly welcome the report by mr brunetta, which proposes appropriate obligations for operators and insists that ex ante regulation be abolished as soon as the objectives set have been achieved through market mechanisms. such interventions will be necessary as long as the former monopoly holders, who still provide most of the connections, still exist. limited resources controlled by one or a few operators, such as the local connection network in telecommunications, are another current obstacle to competition.

- mr president, i have to go. madam president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the historic task of the union will be difficult to solve, but it is very simple to name it. the most urgent task is the establishment of a european democracy. that is the heart of our dispute and our conflict. the greatest obstacle to european democracy - it must be said - is the council's claim to power, the claim to power of the national governments, which want to be not only governments in this europe, not only legislators, but also constitutional creators of europe. the council of nice has failed in its role as the constituent body of europe. this was the arrogance of power which jeopardizes the acceptance of european unity by the citizens. the commission's position is the most important open question in this debate on european democracy. madam president, you spoke of transparency, of citizens' proximity, of dialogue, of parliaments as the source of ideas, of large public debates, and you said that you thought you were talking about democracy. you didn't say anything about democracy! democracy in europe requires that we talk about the fundamental principles and elements of democracy, about the separation of powers, about the intolerability of national administrations making laws. democracy demands that we talk about the principle of the publicity of legislation, that it is intolerable that the council decides on laws behind closed doors and the citizens no longer have the right to know who has decided what and why, and no longer can hold those who make the decisions to account. democracy requires that we talk about the catalogue of fundamental rights, about legal binding and about citizens' access to the court, that we talk about a system of checks and balances and that the right to give constitutions is never and nowhere and in no democracy the right of governments, but that it is the original, indispensable task of parliaments. until the council renounces its claim to power and puts these elements of democracy at the centre of the debate, we shall not be able to solve this great historical problem! what are you standing for today? i was taught in school that parliament is to control and the government is to govern. where do you rule? the european parliament has always been a party to the treaty of rome, and it has always been a party to the treaty of rome. but what does the council stand for today? why not simply say that they want a genuinely democratic and transparent europe with all the consequences? is it so hard for you to take responsibility for your constituents? what's going on in sweden? we admire your country - i am a fan of your political transparency, and now i lack the courage to address these key questions for the future. if you do not lead and only seduce, and then also the wrong bride, namely the national, backward-looking and not the radiant future-oriented bride europe, you risk going down in history as the gravedigger of europe, because you could not explain to your citizens and wanted to explain that the european union plays an indispensable role in the age of globalisation and that this european union must be fundamentally democratized and subject to the principle of subsidiarity. i wish sweden had some courage. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only institution in the world to have a right to vote. i trust your ambition. the economy in europe be made in this context. it 's been cut once today . the forecasts for the current year and the analysis of the status quo are generally positive, but of course they also contain some risk elements, which i shall deal with later. the positive aspect of all the criticisms and suggestions for improvement is that we have a relatively high growth rate in gross domestic product. on the positive side, the unemployment rate is falling and we have created 2.5 million new jobs in the european union in recent years. the improvement in public finances and the associated reduction in the inflation rate and the price stability that has prevailed for years are positive. we have that on the side of the barn. but there are two elements that should be noted and that can cause concern: on the one hand, this is the economic situation in the united states. growth is declining in the united states, which could have a negative impact on the eu economy given the impact of strong us growth on european trade, and it is also positive that we are becoming more independent of economic developments in america, which is also a positive sign and a positive testimony to our actions. the second element is the slowdown in the european economy. the forecasts foresee an average growth rate of 2.8%. i would therefore like to make it very clear that we must once again appeal to all member states to implement the will to reform more vigorously and to make structural measures sustainable. we must criticize the fact that consolidation efforts have stalled in some countries. i would simply point out that the consolidation efforts are all the more critical in that the majority of countries have still not met the self-imposed requirements of the stability and growth pact, despite the favourable economic situation. a second area to be addressed is the fact that we have to congratulate the ecb on the fact that the policy of the quiet hand has proved to be the right one. the ecb must continue to pursue a stability-oriented policy and may continue to pursue an independent european stability-oriented policy. we have some demands, too. the inflation rate must be kept below 2% in the second half of the year, and a balanced liberalisation of the telecommunications, postal, energy and railway sectors must be rapidly implemented. we need to make a greater effort in education and training. we need to increase the flexibility of the labour market and accelerate structural reforms in the goods, services, capital and labour markets. mr president, let me conclude by saying: the main thrust of the karl von wogau report is also to point to the common principles which can be derived from our organisational principle of the social market economy, i.e. freedom and democracy, competition, price stability and sustainable growth, subsidiarity, solidarity and private property, and the responsibility not only of employers towards their employees but of both towards the future and thus the further development of the social market economy towards an eco-social market economy.
i am not going to go into the details of the matter. the development of the european economy can still be described as good. but we also know that we expected more. less encouraging is the fact that inflation may again exceed the 2% threshold and investment may fall from 5.3% to 4.9% of gdp. for this reason, we must continue to ensure that the important european objectives of promoting small and medium-sized enterprises, cutting red tape and reducing the excessive tax rate are also implemented consistently. but we also need to invest in our education systems and reform them so that we don't actually have to feel the threat of a shortage of specialists. in the end, only economic development can be considered successful if it also succeeds in reducing the far too high unemployment rate in europe.

service will lead to a change of mind in this respect. what do we learn from these olaf reports? even for someone who has been dealing with this matter for a long time, it is always surprising to see the extent and sophistication of fraud being organized across borders across europe at the expense of the eu budget. let's just take the example of the butter that's been pan-fried. this mixture of bovine manure and chemical substances was produced under the control of the mafia in italy, but was marketed mainly by companies in france, and in some cases by way of a company in london, to further conceal and cover up this large-scale fraud. all this was, so to speak, the prerequisite for this fraud, which was unprecedented in its nature and scale, to be able to function for years. or take the so-called flax case. at first, it was only about spain. then we had to discover that other member states were also affected and that in the end the fraud in spain was only possible because a company in belgium had issued acceptance certificates for fictitious deliveries. i am glad that, despite the intense political debate in spain over this case, we can say that the cooperation between the spanish authorities and olaf seems to be working well and is characterised by the will to investigate the events and to punish those responsible. the weakest point - and this too must be said clearly and unambiguously - is often still olaf's inability to investigate internal irregularities. we see this most clearly in the case of the allegedly missing protocols in the fléchard affair. this case has been handled with the utmost care. i can relate. if you look at this case with all the consequences, it could turn out that high and senior officials have simply and poignantly lied. that's an explanation, but it's not a justification. if olaf investigates quickly and efficiently, as in the case of the stockholm delegation, then the commission struggles strangely with the necessary disciplinary consequences. this is why the call for a rapid entry into the first stage of the european public prosecutor is so important. we need the so-called internal prosecutor so that the mr president, like my predecessors, i think that we should take the case of mr sikhrovsky, which we are discussing today, as an opportunity to think beyond the mere routine treatment of the meaning and absurdity of parliamentary immunity. the historical roots of the immunity, namely the free mandate of members of parliament to protect them against the interference of a power which was often still monarchical in its origins, can now only partly justify the institution of immunity. there can be no doubt that for statements made in the direct exercise of the mandate and for votes in this house, the factual immunity - also known as indemnity - must be fully guaranteed today. but when it comes to actions and statements which are becoming increasingly political, and when it is not a question of protection from state prosecution but of the rights of citizens vis-à-vis members of parliament, unreasoned immunity is now reaching the limits of its legitimacy. that's when immunity becomes a privilege. if we in the european parliament, unlike many national parliaments, still grant very wide protection, then each individual member should behave in a way that allows us to actually maintain this practice. the generosity of our parliament, which is also the result of considerations of principle in this case - and that is how it should be - also benefits members who belong to parties which do not take the protection of freedom of expression and information very seriously at home.

mr president, according to the international atomic energy agency, the number of thyroid cancer cases has increased sharply. this is stated in a report by the otto-hug-institute of the university of munich. the who predicts that in the gomel district of belarus alone, the main affected area, one third of the children who were under four years old at the time of the accident will develop thyroid cancer in their lifetime. in seven million people, the effects could be felt for the next 50 to 70 years. in belarus alone, 800 million euros are missing annually for the treatment of radiation victims. they don't treat people over 45 for lack of money. that means their certain death! the commission's budget for the sixth eu nuclear research programme, which is 1.2 billion euros, is a sad balance sheet. the european union is called upon to adopt uniform, stringent nuclear safety standards. a second chernobyl must be prevented. therefore, no sham environmental tests should be carried out as in temelin!
the commission has already done so. the chernobyl disaster would not have been considered possible before, even in a dictatorship. of course, i do not believe that this can happen in any british, french or german nuclear power plant. but there are a lot of nuclear power plants around us with outdated russian technology. i only think of bohunice, koslodui and ignalina. they are considered to be beyond reprocessing, even with the very best western technology. in austria there is no one, no party, who is in favour of using nuclear energy. we've learned to accept that we can't force anyone to do what we do. but we can do one thing: we have the right to warn against dangerous nuclear power stations, which would not be authorized to operate in any country of the european union. when someone builds a house today, they ask the neighbors, is the tree in the garden too high, does it give too much shade? if someone runs a nuclear power station which would not be licensed in any of the applicant countries, then you talk about national sovereignty, you are offended when you are asked about safety standards and when you are asked for information. we austrians - i think i can really say this for all of us - have long been calling for uniform standards to be applied in all european countries. i would like to ask you, commissioner, to do so twice today. i know, for example, that french technology may not be comparable to british technology, and that french industry may not want to share its secrets with the rest of the world. but there should be a minimum of consensus. it should be possible to say whether an old or new nuclear power station being built in a candidate country - and i think temelin is quite clear on this - is safe. i can say that if a nuclear power plant is not authorized to operate in any country of the european union, i am against having a nuclear power plant in an applicant country which does not meet our standards, whatever country it is! i think we are harmonizing so much in the european union - our common values, thank god! we will soon have a common currency, wonderful! we also talk about the joint curvature of cucumbers. why is it not possible to reach a minimum consensus here? mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, who are still here! the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of drugs. but unfortunately, you are god's problem, and not just a problem, but the problem itself. the fact is that the use of drugs, which is now so widespread, is threatening to destroy a part of our youth and, as a result, a part of our society, and to undermine it through organized crime. drugs are only helping a few in the field of organized crime to make big money, but on the other hand they are destroying many lives and many existences. i would like to add some figures to what i have just said, which have been made available to us by europol and the drugs monitoring centre in lisbon. it is estimated that some 8 000 tonnes of cannabis and derivatives, 350 tonnes of cocaine, 60 tonnes of heroin and at least 50 million ecstasy tablets are placed on the european market each year. these are the latest figures available. all of this is being consumed, and increasingly by young people. when you know that studies show that about 20% of young people have used cannabis at least once and that an estimated 5 million young people use synthetic drugs, alarm bells must ring. the producers, the dealers, who deal in these drugs are part of organized crime. they're running their business at the expense of the youth. we must therefore do everything possible to combat this crime - which is part of organized crime, as i said - in the union and internationally, with representatives from the other countries of the world which are also affected. i therefore welcome this swedish initiative, which takes us a step further. it's a puzzle in the overall toolbox that we need to be able to survive here to some extent. the first initiative, which concerns the transfer of drugs for the purpose of drug analysis and testing, is designed to avoid abuse by transporting large quantities, but is very suitable as a tool in the fight against drugs. secondly, i think it is particularly important that analyses be used to verify the origin of synthetic drugs. both instruments are essential in the fight against drugs and organised crime. but what needs to happen is that the network of these laboratories, which have the appropriate standardisation and quality, needs to be extended. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. so there should be no separate or parallel work, but it should be done together in cooperation with europol. the third point which i think is very important is this: although the european union has now become the export market for synthetic drugs and the candidate countries are the recipient countries - they have also become transit countries, they have become destination countries - we must now involve these candidate countries intensively with all the instruments at our disposal in order to build a greater security area, which is our aim. the aim, and i know it's far-reaching, is a drug-free society. it will be difficult to achieve, but with these steps we are undoubtedly getting a little closer to the goal.

- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i congratulate my colleague mr casaca on this excellent report. it shows that the olive oil regulation has still not been adapted to the requirements of modern agriculture. the need for a fundamental reform of this agricultural policy is once again evident. i would like to give two examples. firstly, one of the main objectives of production aid is to provide olive growers with a fair income and to ensure a market balance. the court of auditors rightly observes that 'the objective of production aid in terms of producer income has never been defined'. this means that no one knows whether this goal was ever achieved, because it was never possible to evaluate it. the same applies to market equilibrium. in 1998, the commission estimated that there were 180 million olive trees. this was a surprise at the time, because olistat calculated with much fewer trees, about 140 million. that's a difference of 40 million trees, that's about a 31% variance. imagine for a moment that you think you're in equilibrium, and then you realize you have 30% too much stock! in fact, the increase in stocks over the last two years indicates a surplus. secondly, the consumer aid which was abolished at the end of 1998 showed similar weaknesses. records, accounts receivable, were not required to be kept. the result is a high level of irregularity and fraud. the recovery of amounts unduly paid for the period 1985-98 still stands at eur 429 million. only 6% has been recovered so far. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. there's a lot to do here. as regards export refunds, only 13% of the funds were recovered. if you are serious about protecting the taxpayers of the european union, you will start reforming this agricultural policy. the report by mr casaca shows the way.

insurance undertakings. i should like to comment on both proposals at the same time, since they have many similarities. these two proposals, which form part of the action plan for financial services adopted in lisbon and to be implemented by 2005, strengthen and improve the existing rules on non-life insurance adopted in 1973 and on life insurance adopted in 1979. the solvency margin is an additional capital reserve which insurance companies must create as a buffer against unforeseen events. the general aim of the proposals is to achieve better protection of insurance undertakings through higher equity capital of insurance companies and more efficient state supervision of insurance companies. higher solvency margins or risk cushions not only increase confidence in the ability of insurance companies to perform, but also reduce systemic risks in financial markets in general. efficient insurance undertakings play an important role in perfecting and exploiting the potential opportunities of the internal market. the directives provide a basic framework of mandatory conditions and controls and leave room for national legislators to lay down rules beyond this level of regulation. this is absolutely in line with the principle of subsidiarity and, given the considerable cultural differences in the supervisory laws of the member states, it is also understandable and positive from the consumer's point of view. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. this preventive surveillance is also contained in basel ii. this document was proposed by the bank's basel committee on international settlement for banking supervision. the commission proposal on small mutual insurance groups is approved. although the limit for exemption from these directives has been raised from an annual contribution of eur 500 000 to eur 5 million over the last three years, it is ensured that these schemes do not drive insurance companies out of the market. these mutual societies are often local or regional in nature and may remain so. however, if these insurance companies also want a pan-european concession to operate internationally, a simple notification to the national supervisory authority that they voluntarily submit to the provisions of the solvency directive is sufficient. this is an absolute guarantee of the survival of the associations, and it is up to each association to expand its business or not. the mr president, this directive is a major step towards the kyoto target and a milestone on the road to a sustainable energy economy and energy policy in the european union. the rapporteur deserves our thanks and congratulations. but with the prospect of resource scarcity, the rising price of oil and gas, and the risk of nuclear power, there is no reason to sit back and gloat. we must make it clear to the member states of the european union that there are economic and social advantages to achieving the objectives set. of course i would have preferred to have binding targets, but we must now do even more persuasive work to give renewable energy a chance on the market, namely a voluntary one. another long-term goal is to have all forms of energy labelled, because the energy consumer has the right to know from which primary energy source his electricity comes.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would also like to join the ranks of those who congratulate mrs rothe on her inclusion of the binding objectives, because the probability of reaching these binding objectives is very high. i believe that it is also necessary to ensure that the cost is shared efficiently and fairly within the european union, so that the promotion of renewable energy is also comparable and follows the same standards. i would ask the commission to do a lot of best practice and benchmarking because it is very important for member states, and especially for new entrants, to know where the best and worst values are. i also believe that small and safe cycles are important for the future of europe and for security of supply, and as we have seen with water cycles, they are actually the best and cleanest way of producing energy. i think that this will open up a lot of new possibilities. if the transmission system operators now ensure that there is no discrimination and that renewable energy is given priority, we can be very satisfied. this directive is of course an intermediate step, but i believe it is a proper intermediate step. i hope that the commission will soon present a general certification model with which we can then continue to work. i think that the commission has a duty to ensure that the directive is implemented in a way that is consistent with the principles of the treaty.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i too join in congratulating the rapporteur. i would like to make a few comments, however. the fact that no binding targets have been reached has been a matter of concern to us all, but we can also take a cue from the various un conventions. i think of rio, where i was able to follow in detail, as a correspondent myself, that something big can grow from something small. the commission's double-mindedness gives us the opportunity to take the initiative and then propose binding objectives. i would like to ask the commissioner present here to do so as a precaution. the successful systems can also be used as a model. spain, germany and austria already have the facilities and the ways to deal with renewable energies that others can take over. the aim is not to make the world heal from the german nature, but simply to save unnecessary additional expenditure, which then leads to excuses that the objectives could not have been achieved. the rapporteur's suggestion that proofs of origin will ensure transparency is in line with my ceterum censeo, which is that the european institutions must be fully transparent for the benefit of all citizens.
mr president, after a rather technical introduction, we should not lose sight of what is at stake. it is often rightly said that europe has no face. if you want to give yourself an address as a publicist and entrepreneur, it's dot.at, dot.dot.com, dot.dot.net, dot.org. now we get a face, we get an address. it is worth something if we put it together and if the commission finds the appropriate way. we become dot.eu. the fact that we are so late has a lot to do with the fact that we have not yet managed to grow together into the unity that we pretend to have already grown together. this europe is gradually developing from the single market, perhaps in a hopeful way, into identity through politics. we know there's a lot going wrong. i think it is a step forward that there is a certain marking associated with dot.eu. i am counting on the commission and all those responsible to ensure that there will be no sloppiness when we get this main domain, that there can be incidents such as those mentioned by the previous speakers. the keyword is "amsterdam dot.nl". i would also like, however, in the course of enlargement and also of the re-regionalisation of europe, that the regions also have the chance to find appropriate additions or priorities. i would not agree with the present report on this point. however, i welcome the rapporteur's reference to the principle of transparency in amendment no 15, which brings me to my ceterum censeo, that the eu institutions must be fully transparent for the benefit of their citizens.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. europe is number one in the world in terms of gross domestic product and i think it is good that we continue to make a little more effort in marketing for europe in the future. i welcome the fact that we want to become the most competitive area in the world, and i believe we still have to work hard to achieve this. the competitors in this world are not sleeping, but they are running good initiatives, so it is important for us to act as efficiently and quickly as possible in this area. we have 18 million businesses in europe, and i think it is a very important marketing advantage for these businesses to be identifiable in a uniform way, immediately and without any problems, all over the world. consumer confidence is the essential basis for business, and therefore the presence of the european union in the global networks with a top label domain is of particular importance. i believe that there is also a need for efficient dispute settlement, which should be as cost-effective as possible, but which should also be neutral and non-discriminatory. speculation or the abusive registration of names should be tackled as quickly and efficiently as possible, because this is a very important basis for the partners involved and can prompt them to act efficiently. the fact that the register is being advertised is also very important because it also ensures optimum supply in the european area, and i wish the commission and in particular mrs flesch all the best. i believe that you have taken an important step in this european union.

the rapporteur. i am glad to say that the commission has taken the initiative in this respect. there is no doubt that the european parliament is supporting and evaluating positively and constructively the accession process of malta, this small mediterranean island at the crossroads between the european union and africa. the report is based on the copenhagen criteria, which apply equally to all the applicant countries, and on the specific features of malta, which are due to its insularity and its economic and social structure. the basic political situation in malta has not changed. malta continues to meet the political criteria of copenhagen. its institutions are democratic and function smoothly. there are no particular problems with regard to human rights. the overall situation of economic, social and cultural rights is satisfactory. in the economic field, malta has a functioning market economy and is able to withstand eu competition in the medium term, provided it carries out the structural reforms still to be implemented. malta is on track in this area, both in terms of reducing import duties on finished products by 2003 and in terms of eliminating existing capital controls by the end of 2002. the broad privatisation programme adopted in 1999, including major utilities over the next five years, is also pointing in the right direction. the reduction of the budget deficit from 11.8% of gdp in 1998 to 5% of gdp this year is a positive and significant result, as is the positive development of the economic data. the inflation rate was 2.4% in november 2000, despite the oil price developments, and the unemployment rate fell to 5.3% of the working population, after a slight increase in 1999. however, it must be borne in mind that this positive process must be consolidated by further reductions in structural expenditure. malta's positive development on the path to the eu is also reflected in the state of accession negotiations. with 17 chapters, malta is at the top of the list of candidates who were only able to start negotiations in 2000. this shows that malta has made up for the lost time of the 22 months' break in the negotiations and is therefore in a good position to be among the first round of new accession countries. the basic political situation in malta, namely the different assessment of eu accession by the government on the one hand and the opposition on the other, has not only remained unchanged, but the polarization between the government and the labour opposition has rather become even more acute. as rapporteur, i can only note this with regret. this does not mean, of course, that the european parliament ignores the position of the labour party, which wants to take a swiss approach to malta. however, all possibilities must be used to ensure the continuity of malta's accession process and thus to support a constructive dialogue with all constituent bodies and social strata in malta. the european parliament's particular concerns in relation to malta's accession process naturally include internal security and maritime security. as regards internal security, malta has now set the stage for an effective anti-corruption and anti-money laundering policy which will allow malta to accede to both the council of europe criminal law convention against corruption and to ratify the oecd convention on the fight against bribery of foreign officials in international business, thus addressing a short-term priority of the accession partnership. in the environmental field, too, we support malta's efforts to support the short-term priorities of the accession partnership on water and air pollution and the reduction of landfills, and therefore express our understanding of malta's policy on banning the use of plastic bottles. as rapporteur, i have also shown my understanding of malta's specific concerns regarding the free movement of persons and its fears about an influx of foreign workers into its labour market. the commission's concession on the free movement of persons takes account of this rather psychological fear. malta is not a transformation society. it has repeatedly stated that it wants to be treated like the other candidates for accession. the commission's efforts to open up financial channels to make up for this shortfall are to be welcomed. on the other hand, the maltese authorities must make appropriate preparations to enable the management of structural and regional aid to be decentralised. i just want to take a moment to address the council. the position of malta on the agricultural chapter should also, i hope, be on the table soon. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us. five points from the point of view of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. firstly, we are all aware of the historical dimension and the political and moral responsibility of the reunification of europe. it must succeed and will succeed because it is right and it makes sense. secondly, the economic facts are developing positively. economic growth in central and eastern europe remains above the eu average. the macroeconomic situation has improved. eu exports to the candidate countries are increasing, and the candidate countries are already benefiting from the accession negotiation process because it also provides them with guidance for necessary reforms that would have to be carried out even without an eu perspective after 50 years of communist dictatorship. thirdly, the commissioner has cut it: the economic and monetary union, the euro, has not yet been achieved with accession. it is important to point out that once the copenhagen criteria have been met, the maastricht criteria will have to be met without an opt-out clause and this timeframe is likely to take at least another three, four, five or more years. fourthly, money and balance sheets are not everything. the economic data are positive, but they are only a means to an end and not an end in themselves. we have said it today in the middle east debate: means to an end, to create peace, to provide security, to guarantee social stability, to build a democratic community and to safeguard growth and employment. fifthly, there is much to be done: to meet the stability criteria, to control state aid, to eliminate regional disparities, to reform the pension, social and health systems, to speed up privatization and to guarantee the independence of the judiciary and the public administration. we have reached a milestone. we are optimistic about the future.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the proposal which has come from the council represents an opportunity for refugees to be granted refugee status under the geneva convention, according to uniform criteria which apply throughout the union. this council proposal guarantees swift and secure procedures for genuine refugees - that is, about 10% of all those who apply for asylum - and it also guarantees swift rejection. that's about 90 percent of people who have applied for reasons other than actual reasons for fleeing, and that's better than waiting years and then having to face a negative decision. this proposal also guarantees an end to asylum shopping. but we need complementary measures. the dublin convention, eurodac, the rules for safe third countries and safe countries of origin. we, the european people's party, therefore support this proposal from the council. i would also like to make it clear on behalf of the epp that the right of asylum cannot be used politically to encourage immigration for any number of reasons, through the amendments and proposals which have been tabled here. this is a real-life investigation. we must use the instruments we now have as europe to provide for immigration, asylum or temporary assistance when refugees come from war zones. we must not impose unrealistic measures. it is necessary to guarantee in hinkunft that the right to asylum remains reserved for those who are politically persecuted and is not extended to groups that some here want politically, which is not in any way in line with the actual situation under the geneva convention. the aim must be to ensure that we can provide optimal and rapid assistance to genuine refugees and follow-up measures for their integration, but also to make it clear to everyone else that there will be new instruments for immigration to the european union, including for economic reasons.

- madam president, mr president of the commission, mr president! thank you very much for your report today. from time to time, we draw closer together and share a common viewpoint. but it 's not yet time . there's one major point where i think we're different. firstly, it is clear that you have today committed yourself to parliamentary democracy in a form which naturally has important consequences for all our work, and in particular for the role of so-called civil society. this is another somewhat nebulous term, as is governance, but governance is used where parliamentary democracies do not exist or can hardly exist, as in the context of global governance. the civil society - and i think you have made this clear - we must insist on it, it has an advisory, a supporting and a helping role, but at the heart of democracy as we understand it is the european parliament and, of course, the national parliaments. they are, as you said, the expression of the will of the people, and we shall insist on that. the main point, where there are still differences, is the question of alternative forms of regulation. we are not so arrogant as to believe that only parliament can regulate, that only parliament can create appropriate regulations, even in detail. the question of what the framework is, what the details are, what the political direction is, what the technical implementation is - that is a very delicate question! i must therefore insist that we as parliament should always be consulted on this matter and that it should not be the commission that decides on it, but that it should be a joint decision, a kind of interinstitutional agreement between the council, parliament and the commission. this is particularly true of the callback position which we have also dealt with today. to put it very clearly, we need both. we need a call back from parliament on alternative forms of regulation and we need a reform of comitology. we can make friends with what is indicated in the white paper, namely a reform of the comitology, but that is not enough. this is a necessary but not sufficient condition for the decisionmaking process at parliamentary level to become more democratic. i hope you have understood correctly that you, mr president, will not put forward anything in laeken without first discussing it with this parliament and obtaining its opinion. you said it in general, that's good; but i can tell by your nods that you mean it, and that's a big improvement over last time. i would like to thank you very much for that. in conclusion, and to make one thing clear, we do not want to interfere with the commission's executive powers as a parliament. but what we reject most profoundly and thoroughly are attempts by the commission - there are such attempts - to interfere in the legislative powers, to appropriate some of the legislative powers. that is what is stated in certain provisions and phrases in the white paper, and we must say no to that. the executive is the commission's, but the legislative is the parliament's and the council's, and we want to insist on that!

what are the main points of my report? it is clear that the european union can only carry out certain tasks in relation to immigration and that a large part of this must remain in the hands of the member states. it must remain for member states to determine, according to their needs and possibilities, which and how many workers will be granted a residence and work permit for the member state. it is also clear that the conditions for integration, such as existing work, such as appropriate housing, must be taken into account in order for integration to take place. it is also important for us in the home affairs committee that we have expectations of immigrants, namely the willingness to integrate and the recognition of the community of values. conversely, it must also be the duty of the member states to provide adequate integration assistance. at union level, we must define the conditions for the entry and residence of these migrants, establish a coherent system for issuing visas, - mr president, madam commissioner, mr president of the council! i think it is very positive that this conference took place in durban, that there were broad discussions, that a resolution was drawn up, that a conclusion was reached. what i sometimes miss in this debate is that too little is done to look to the future, to think about concrete measures to prevent racism from happening, so that it may not happen again. i would like to ask the european union to set a good example, and i would like to point out two areas where we could do this, because it is topical today: asylum and immigration policy. if we succeed in what we are trying to do, namely to implement a uniform asylum policy with minimum standards, where decisions are made safely and quickly, then we will no longer have the situation we have today, with ghettos where asylum seekers have to wait for years, accept illegal work, and exclusion takes place. quick decision leads to quick assistance and quick integration of recognized refugees and prevents this kind of exclusion that is happening now. that would be prevention against racism! i also hope that there will be progress on immigration policy. if the member states, to the best of their ability and provided that there is work and housing and educational opportunities, take in thirdcountry nationals and offer them integration assistance, then this is again a measure against exclusion, against discrimination and for integration, and i believe that we should take such concrete measures in order to be able to be really successful in preventing racism and discrimination. it is an opportunity for our european union! on the one hand, it is about coordination, so that the eurozone can have a strong internal market and make europe more competitive. on the other hand, i would ask you to understand that it is also a question of preserving freedom as the basis for competition within this agreed framework between the member states. we are not all convinced that coordination is a panacea in itself, because of course we also have to talk about where coordination starts. what mechanisms does coordination use and where are the limits to coordination? is the coordination restrictive of the market or is it supportive of the market? we must have this debate, and decisions cannot be taken under the guise of coordination. we therefore say yes to a strengthening of european debates in national parliaments, yes to coordination of the statistical office of the european communities and to a stronger networking with the national statistical institutes, yes to the mutual exchange of information in order to be able to fulfil the common tasks. but of course no to increasing bureaucracy, to new bodies, to additional administrative burdens under the guise of coordination. we need more efficiency and professionalism of the existing in the common interest. this does not necessarily mean that freedom and freedom of choice in the member states are being curtailed. we say no to advance information on tax collection or the use of tax revenue because we believe that there is no legal basis for this either. i have only highlighted these two aspects, which we have to discuss further. we will agree to the report, but not all the details are clear, what exactly we mean by this and what mechanisms are necessary. i would like to say one more thing to mrs berès: it is important that you have linked public finances to the reduction of inflation and thus to the assessment of the financial markets. we need to restructure public finances to provide more investment capital, and i therefore appeal to all those mentioned in the report to do their homework properly and not just to put in cosmetic measures!

- mr president, i have to go. what is transparency? the transparency would be guaranteed, it could and should be argued, if all interested eu citizens were granted access to as many documents of the european institutions as possible and if the public nature of council meetings were also guaranteed. this is the basic idea behind the european transparency initiative, which was launched by a number of parliamentarians and public figures throughout europe and which is only just beginning to take effect on the basis of the current regulation 1049/2001 and the corresponding amendments which we are now making here in parliament. how much transparency do we want? there are still differences of opinion. i personally would like the european transparency formula to be a mixture of the american and the swedish formula, i.e. unlimited access to almost everything, including no register, and the possibility of really controlling and constantly reviewing our activities in parliament, but also, and especially, where the really important decisions are taken, i.e. in the council. unfortunately, there is not yet a majority in this parliament. it is also possible to argue that the european public, in its diversity, is not yet ready. of course, it can be argued that if an italian or an austrian citizen writes a letter to one of the eu institutions, which is then immediately included in a public register, this is something that he did not expect and he would behave differently, whereas for a swede this would be a matter of course. i think we must continue along this path, especially now, when it is a question of defining european values, also in the light of current events in the world. transparency is a key part of that. what we have now is a step in that direction. the decision-making process in the committee on constitutional affairs has already been mentioned. i would like to see amendment no 15 from the green group substantially more clearly justified than it has been so far. i personally would have no problem with submitting the documents required for budgetary control or with what is required at the end. but if it's against itself, it's not. on amendment no 17, my colleague mr cashman has already said the same thing. amendment no 14 is also fully supported by our group. that's an improvement. the abolition of the limitation to 'permanent' committees in the first section of amendment no 15 is also welcome. we must all be aware that without transparency there can be no democracy. until then, we have a good chunk of work to do.

in writing. -the shortcomings of the present agricultural policy are obvious. the present system only encourages the transport of animals. in some ways, this trade is really absurd. spain, for example, imports sheep for slaughter from the united kingdom, even though it also exports sheep for slaughter in greece. the aim must be to put an end to the encouragement of the transport of live animals. in the long run, the promotion of this practice by european funds is not justifiable. the only way to put an end to the transport of live animals over long distances is to regionalise agricultural policy. the slogan must be to produce within a given market for a given market. in addition, the risk of spreading animal diseases could be effectively controlled in this way.
